AI Media Scout

2026-11-16 · All platforms · Open-source software

34 complete documentation guides and 56 additional screened creative-AI entries, including three GitLab-hosted projects. 9 earlier screened tools now have guides; these are completions, not new launch claims. Concrete workflows include editable type design, packaging vectors/folding previews, footage ingest, original tracker music, narrated film, multiview/4D reconstruction and shadow-puppet interaction. Every admission has a complete current software-grant review and six explicit quality questions; none was installed or run. All newly admitted entries remain Quality unverified or specifically cited Experimental. This Monday’s source review covers all 2919 previously published entries across every tier/platform/source host, with archived current sources and actual failed attempts. Nine existing labels change: six stale/unsupported Experimental verdicts move to Quality unverified, and three receive current specific Experimental reasons. The six higher-confidence entries retain their scoped labels after deeper current checks. Unavailable sources reopen affected checks to unknown; review coverage is not quality approval. Current restrictions/closed-source boundaries are disclosed without rewriting historical editions. The full effective discovery plan covers 107 fields, 1036 repository queries and 25 model tasks, including a new shadow-performance field, adaptive terms and deeper/date-partitioned pagination. Live primary research supplements every field and all eight configured ecosystems. Metadata-level searches covered the complete current and retained discovery catalogs, including historical pilot records, followed by targeted document/license/code reviews beyond the README shortlist. Remaining raw unscreened and excluded candidates stay local. Eight complete research PDFs were captured; selected abstracts/front matter were read for field context, not claimed as full-paper quality review. Direct GitLab raw URLs and older topic sorting had 429 gaps, while official APIs and a latest-activity listing supplied current project evidence. Codeberg searches remained throttled/robots-limited and some project/manual URLs failed. Exact attempt/bounded-source counts follow from verified collection. No star floor, fixed admission quota, platform preference, app execution or additional scheduler is used. Verified collection: 1036/1036 repository queries attempted; 1033 successful, 3 partial, 0 failed and 137 bounded/truncated. All 25 model tasks were attempted; 0 failed. These are bounded discovery attempts, not an exhaustive census or quality approval. Empty model-source responses: text2text-generation. An empty response does not establish that these creative workflows have no tools. Separate from successful search queries, 52 watchlist fetches and 7 enrichment attempts failed; the detailed warnings remain in the report. A 404/409 is an availability gap, not proof of abandonment or license revocation.

Typography, fonts & layout · Images & design

Punchcutter

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-11-13. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 3 GitHub stars · Release v1.3.1 (2026-11-15) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Developing original Latin wordmarks and font families, inspecting spacing and kerning, or turning authorized print handwriting into static fonts with editable UFO sources.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The maintainer supplies five-family specimens, Studio screenshots, a prebuilt Pomelo Round specimen and photo-to-font examples. The demo handwriting is explicitly simulated. These document the workflow without independently establishing professional font quality. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Use Python 3.10+ and a supported coding agent or MCP client. The documented MCP package installs from the official Git repository; register the resulting punchcutter mcp command in the chosen client. On macOS, proof images and handwriting sheets additionally need Cairo. Agent-host setup differs by client. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: Inspected the titles/states in up to ten recently updated issue/PR records and selected first-hand report bodies; 0 records returned. This bounded sample, owner PRs and repository activity do not establish sustained support, closed blockers or independent reliability. [4]
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: Toolkit code is MIT. Example families and open-base remixes retain SIL OFL 1.1; generated original families follow the creator-selected terms. AI-host services and rights to supplied handwriting/reference images remain separate. Local Python computation plus any chosen AI-agent subscription/API charges. No universal zero-cost AI-host claim is established. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Drawn output is Latin-only, upright sans/slab; slant is oblique, not a true italic. Handwriting is print-style/static, not joined cursive. Outlines are unhinted, and a type designer still needs to refine curves/kerning. Anonymous telemetry is documented with an opt-out. No app was run.

How it uses AI

An agent-facing type-design toolkit lets a visual AI turn a brief or handwriting reference into parameter changes, inspect proof images, critique glyphs and rebuild an editable font family. Its font compiler is deterministic; the AI design/critique loop is the creative integration. [1] [3]

Introduction

An agent-facing type-design toolkit lets a visual AI turn a brief or handwriting reference into parameter changes, inspect proof images, critique glyphs and rebuild an editable font family. Its font compiler is deterministic; the AI design/critique loop is the creative integration. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Developing original Latin wordmarks and font families, inspecting spacing and kerning, or turning authorized print handwriting into static fonts with editable UFO sources. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The maintainer supplies five-family specimens, Studio screenshots, a prebuilt Pomelo Round specimen and photo-to-font examples. The demo handwriting is explicitly simulated. These document the workflow without independently establishing professional font quality. [1] [3]

Install

Use Python 3.10+ and a supported coding agent or MCP client. The documented MCP package installs from the official Git repository; register the resulting punchcutter mcp command in the chosen client. On macOS, proof images and handwriting sheets additionally need Cairo. Agent-host setup differs by client. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use Python 3.10+ and a supported coding agent or MCP client. The documented MCP package installs from the official Git repository; register the resulting punchcutter mcp command in the chosen client. On macOS, proof images and handwriting sheets additionally need Cairo. Agent-host setup differs by client.
pip install "punchcutter[mcp] @ git+https://github.com/byrishi/punchcutter"
punchcutter mcp

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Ask for an original family with a name, mood and intended use; select a drawn, handwriting or open-base route.
  2. Inspect proof sheets and individual glyphs, then ask the agent to revise curves, weight, spacing or kerning.
  3. Build the family and inspect the QA report; open the generated Studio to test words and language coverage.
  4. Keep the UFO/design sources and export only after human typographic review. Preserve OFL notices and renamed-family requirements for open-font remixes.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Python 3.10+; package dependencies; an image-capable agent for visual critique, or the deterministic CLI; Cairo on macOS for proofs/sheets.
Platforms
Python and client routes are documented; font-install instructions cover Mac, Windows and Linux. This does not independently verify every client/OS combination.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: Toolkit code is MIT. Example families and open-base remixes retain SIL OFL 1.1; generated original families follow the creator-selected terms. AI-host services and rights to supplied handwriting/reference images remain separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Toolkit code is MIT. Example families and open-base remixes retain SIL OFL 1.1; generated original families follow the creator-selected terms. AI-host services and rights to supplied handwriting/reference images remain separate.
  • Cost: Local Python computation plus any chosen AI-agent subscription/API charges. No universal zero-cost AI-host claim is established.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3]

Limitations

Drawn output is Latin-only, upright sans/slab; slant is oblique, not a true italic. Handwriting is print-style/static, not joined cursive. Outlines are unhinted, and a type designer still needs to refine curves/kerning. Anonymous telemetry is documented with an opt-out. No app was run. [1] [3]

Unableton

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · Apache-2.0 · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-11-14. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 0 GitHub stars · Release v0.0.1 (2026-11-16) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Experimental

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Sketching editable arrangements through chat, correcting notes in a piano roll and exporting stereo WAV or multitrack MIDI on a supported Mac.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Why Experimental: The current application is explicitly a development preview; signed/notarized distribution and accessibility/platform acceptance remain unfinished for the assessed music-editor workflow. [1] [3]
  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The screenshots show a locally prepared arrangement and an unsent refinement prompt, explicitly not a recorded AI-generation run. The documented draft/validate/commit diagram is evidence of intended behavior, not independently verified musical results. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Obtain the official Apple Silicon development-preview DMG for macOS 26+. Source builds additionally need Swift 6.2+ and Xcode command-line tools; the published build-and-run script builds and ad-hoc signs the app. In Settings, supply an OpenAI key, test the connection and choose a fixed model or the Automatic routing mode. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: Inspected the titles/states in up to ten recently updated issue/PR records and selected first-hand report bodies; 0 records returned. This bounded sample, owner PRs and repository activity do not establish sustained support, closed blockers or independent reliability. [4]

No sampled banks or demo audio are bundled. Rinoa integration terms are explicitly unresolved. Manual editing, built-in playback and single-layer faithful local transcription can run offline; cloud composition uses the user’s separately billed OpenAI account. Optional services have separate access and billing. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Development preview: signing/notarization, complete VoiceOver acceptance and macOS 27 validation remain unfinished. One project plays/records at a time. Local detection is monophonic, does not transcribe lyrics and can err. Third-party plugins, external MIDI recording, advanced time stretching and comping are outside this release. Default credential storage is an owner-only unencrypted local file; Keychain is an explicit option.

How it uses AI

A native music editor converts cloud-LLM requests into typed edits of tracks, MIDI-like notes, arrangements and instrument settings. An AI edit is committed as one undoable project change; manual music editing and local playback remain available. [1] [3]

Introduction

A native music editor converts cloud-LLM requests into typed edits of tracks, MIDI-like notes, arrangements and instrument settings. An AI edit is committed as one undoable project change; manual music editing and local playback remain available. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Sketching editable arrangements through chat, correcting notes in a piano roll and exporting stereo WAV or multitrack MIDI on a supported Mac. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The screenshots show a locally prepared arrangement and an unsent refinement prompt, explicitly not a recorded AI-generation run. The documented draft/validate/commit diagram is evidence of intended behavior, not independently verified musical results. [1] [3]

Install

Obtain the official Apple Silicon development-preview DMG for macOS 26+. Source builds additionally need Swift 6.2+ and Xcode command-line tools; the published build-and-run script builds and ad-hoc signs the app. In Settings, supply an OpenAI key, test the connection and choose a fixed model or the Automatic routing mode. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Obtain the official Apple Silicon development-preview DMG for macOS 26+. Source builds additionally need Swift 6.2+ and Xcode command-line tools; the published build-and-run script builds and ad-hoc signs the app. In Settings, supply an OpenAI key, test the connection and choose a fixed model or the Automatic routing mode.
git clone https://github.com/VirajShah21/unableton.git
cd unableton
./script/build_and_run.sh

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Start a song and request a short arrangement; keep the first request small and inspect every track.
  2. Select a track or clip for context, refine notes, velocities and sounds, and use Command-Z to undo a whole AI edit.
  3. For performance input, open Transcribe, record/import a monophonic passage, preview detected events and explicitly Add to Song.
  4. Save the .unableton package and verify WAV/MIDI exports. Download only the FreePats banks actually used; optional vocals need separate consent and provider terms.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
macOS 26+; source build Swift 6.2+ and Xcode command-line tools. Built with SwiftUI, AVAudioEngine and C++.
Platforms
The documented release is Apple Silicon on macOS 26+. macOS 27 acceptance is unfinished. Windows/Linux support is not documented.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: Application code is Apache-2.0. OpenAI composition, optional vocal services/Jev review and downloaded FreePats banks retain their own terms. No sampled banks or demo audio are bundled. Rinoa integration terms are explicitly unresolved.
  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. Application code is Apache-2.0. OpenAI composition, optional vocal services/Jev review and downloaded FreePats banks retain their own terms. No sampled banks or demo audio are bundled. Rinoa integration terms are explicitly unresolved.
  • Cost: Manual editing, built-in playback and single-layer faithful local transcription can run offline; cloud composition uses the user’s separately billed OpenAI account. Optional services have separate access and billing.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3]

Limitations

Development preview: signing/notarization, complete VoiceOver acceptance and macOS 27 validation remain unfinished. One project plays/records at a time. Local detection is monophonic, does not transcribe lyrics and can err. Third-party plugins, external MIDI recording, advanced time stretching and comping are outside this release. Default credential storage is an owner-only unencrypted local file; Keychain is an explicit option. [1] [3]

Vertigo

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · first-profile

First complete guide in this library, including completion of any earlier screened entry; not a claim of a launch today.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 0 GitHub stars · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Turning a permitted photograph into a short parallax/dolly-zoom concept loop or a miniature diorama shot, with human review of foreground edges and exposed background areas.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The official static demo and forest example document the intended interface; bundled samples have precomputed depth. They do not demonstrate independent fresh-photo inference or eliminate depth/occlusion artifacts. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Use the official browser demo, or clone the MIT source, install its npm dependencies and start the Vite development server. A fresh photo requires a first model download; WebGPU fp16 is used when available, otherwise WASM q8. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: Inspected the titles/states in up to ten recently updated issue/PR records and selected first-hand report bodies; 0 records returned. This bounded sample, owner PRs and repository activity do not establish sustained support, closed blockers or independent reliability. [4]
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT application code. The named ONNX Depth Anything V2 Small conversion has separate model terms/provenance; Three.js, Transformers.js, fonts and Unsplash sample-photo rights remain separate. Local browser compute and initial model/static-asset downloads; no paid inference service is documented for this selected path. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. This creates a two-plane depth effect, not a complete recovered 3D scene. Background filling is deterministic push–pull processing; inferred depth and hidden surfaces need visual review. Samples can skip model inference because depth is precomputed. Network requests still include the site, fonts and model weights even though photo pixels are processed locally.

How it uses AI

Depth Anything V2 Small estimates a depth map in a browser worker. Two depth-displaced image planes and deterministic background filling then support looping camera motion and relighting of a still photograph. [1] [3]

Introduction

Depth Anything V2 Small estimates a depth map in a browser worker. Two depth-displaced image planes and deterministic background filling then support looping camera motion and relighting of a still photograph. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Turning a permitted photograph into a short parallax/dolly-zoom concept loop or a miniature diorama shot, with human review of foreground edges and exposed background areas. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The official static demo and forest example document the intended interface; bundled samples have precomputed depth. They do not demonstrate independent fresh-photo inference or eliminate depth/occlusion artifacts. [1] [3]

Install

Use the official browser demo, or clone the MIT source, install its npm dependencies and start the Vite development server. A fresh photo requires a first model download; WebGPU fp16 is used when available, otherwise WASM q8. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the official browser demo, or clone the MIT source, install its npm dependencies and start the Vite development server. A fresh photo requires a first model download; WebGPU fp16 is used when available, otherwise WASM q8.
git clone https://github.com/chris-wozniczek/vertigo.git
cd vertigo
npm install
npm run dev

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Drop/paste a permitted photo or choose a sample and wait for depth estimation.
  2. Select Vertigo, Ken Burns, Orbit, Crane, Swing or Window; adjust depth intensity, haze, light, focus and grade.
  3. Inspect depth boundaries throughout the camera move, especially newly exposed background regions.
  4. Export a documented 4–6-second loop in 16:9, square or 9:16 at 1080p, or a PNG. MP4/H.264 availability depends on browser MediaRecorder support; WebM is the fallback.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum RAM/VRAM/storage is not documented. The maintainer reports approximately 25–50 MB of model weights, cached after first download; this is not a complete memory or disk minimum.
Software
A modern browser with WebGPU or the documented WASM fallback; Node/npm for source development. Explicit minimum Node version is not documented.
Platforms
Browser workflow and phone tilt are documented. Desktop OS/version support and uniform mobile export compatibility are not independently established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: MIT application code. The named ONNX Depth Anything V2 Small conversion has separate model terms/provenance; Three.js, Transformers.js, fonts and Unsplash sample-photo rights remain separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT application code. The named ONNX Depth Anything V2 Small conversion has separate model terms/provenance; Three.js, Transformers.js, fonts and Unsplash sample-photo rights remain separate.
  • Cost: Local browser compute and initial model/static-asset downloads; no paid inference service is documented for this selected path.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3]

Limitations

This creates a two-plane depth effect, not a complete recovered 3D scene. Background filling is deterministic push–pull processing; inferred depth and hidden surfaces need visual review. Samples can skip model inference because depth is precomputed. Network requests still include the site, fonts and model weights even though photo pixels are processed locally. [1] [3]

Software
Windows 10/11 x64; separately installed PhotoCraft; OpenCode; stable-diffusion.cpp; selected Qwen image model, encoder, VAE and optional LoRA. Node 20+ is for development.
Platforms

faster-whisper produces speech/word-time drafts that FFmpeg turns into highlighted captions, alongside silence-based cutting and vertical video preparation. [1]

A local Canvas/WebCodecs scene editor optionally uses Groq neural voice and Whisper timing; its default syllable/pause caption timing and procedural soundtrack are distinguished from those model-backed operations. [1]

What it is good for

Making short caption-led videos from a line-by-line script or authorized voice recording, while checking caption timing before export. [1]

Demo & examples

The maintainer describes templates, previews and preflight checks. A local export or synthetic soundtrack is not proof of model-backed voice/timing quality. [1]

Install

Keep the supplied vendor folder beside index.html and open it in a recent desktop Chrome or Edge, or use a local static server. Network access is required for Google Fonts and any selected Groq voice/Whisper request; configure the optional provider privately. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Keep the supplied vendor folder beside index.html and open it in a recent desktop Chrome or Edge, or use a local static server. Network access is required for Google Fonts and any selected Groq voice/Whisper request; configure the optional provider privately.
python -m http.server 8000

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Open the local page, enter one scene per script line and choose a short narration source.
  2. Select optional Groq inference deliberately; otherwise the documented timing is estimated from syllables/pauses.
  3. Check script, captions, safe areas, factual claims and any music/effect choices in the preview.
  4. Export a short MP4/WebM and inspect audio synchronization and actual codec/browser fallback behavior.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
A recent desktop Chrome or Edge, the bundled muxer files, and network access for external fonts/optional inference. A static server is optional; no build step is documented.
Platforms
Desktop Chrome/Edge recommended. Firefox/Safari may use live-recording fallbacks; no uniform mobile/OS-version export guarantee is established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: The complete current root MIT grant was reviewed, despite an unfinished license-reference sentence in the README. Bundled mp4-muxer/webm-muxer, fonts and any Groq models/services have separate terms.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. The complete current root MIT grant was reviewed, despite an unfinished license-reference sentence in the README. Bundled mp4-muxer/webm-muxer, fonts and any Groq models/services have separate terms.
  • Cost: Local rendering plus any optional Groq access/usage and initial font downloads. Browser/system voices and a procedural music option do not establish a free neural inference service.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

AI voice/Whisper requests can transmit selected material to the provider; the rest of the documented render path is local. Estimated syllable timing is not exact forced alignment. The full model/service and independent-result review remains open. [1]

Scope: Exploring a self-hosted music-generation client with an already available compatible ACE-Step endpoint; rebuilding the owner’s hosted service is outside this guide.

  • Reproducible setup · documented: For the independent client, install the current source as a Python package, then run sonora setup, sonora doctor and sonora start. How it uses AI

An installable studio submits described-music jobs to a separately supplied ACE-Step REST engine and keeps durable jobs, a track library and browser assets. Its source also documents a distinct hosted Modal/Google studio. [1] [3]

Introduction

An installable studio submits described-music jobs to a separately supplied ACE-Step REST engine and keeps durable jobs, a track library and browser assets. Its source also documents a distinct hosted Modal/Google studio. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Exploring a self-hosted music-generation client with an already available compatible ACE-Step endpoint; rebuilding the owner’s hosted service is outside this guide. [1] [3]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Select the independent self-hosted client rather than the hosted-UI fixture.
  2. Configure your own compatible engine and inspect doctor/protocol status before a short job.
  3. Submit one small music request, inspect job/library state and listen to the returned file.
  4. Retain settings and track backups. Treat requested genre/tempo/duration as model guidance, not guaranteed exact musical parameters.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
No universal client RAM/VRAM minimum is documented. Experimental Linux/NVIDIA bootstrap asks for at least30 GiB free, with about 10.1 GB decimal pinned model files plus dependencies/caches. Its4 GB DiT-only VRAM figure is an upstream claim, not a tested Sonora minimum. Hosted A10/L4 eight-core32 GiB configuration is a separate route.
Software
Python 3.11 or3.12; tested client environment Linux x86_64/Python 3.12. Installable studio needs no Node, Modal SDK, torch or GPU; real generation requires a separate official ACE-Step1.5 REST server. Arbitrary music/chat APIs and path-prefixed reverse proxies are unsupported.
Platforms
The experimental bootstrap targets Linux/NVIDIA. Browser layout is responsive, but a tested client OS/version matrix is not established. Hosted Google/Modal configuration is a different path.

  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT application code; ACE-Step weights/language model and engine/runtime/service terms are separate. A frontend/source grant does not reproduce the private hosted deployment or provide inference credits.
  • Cost: Your engine hardware or separately billed server/provider access. The self-hosted path supplies no shared owner credits or promise of free inference.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3]

Limitations

The hosted GPU image/pins/verification files are missing from this snapshot, so that deployment is not reproducible from this repository alone. The independent client is alpha and its new clean GPU bootstrap is unvalidated. No mastering, stems, collaboration or guaranteed musical structure is documented. Current alpha has no remote cancellation; uncertain submissions block further work and are not automatically retried. Remotion voiceover

The repository was created on 2026-11-15. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 0 GitHub stars · Release v0.1.0 (2026-11-15) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Revising multi-voice narration without regenerating every line and handing actual audio durations to a code-based video timeline.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]

Separately billed Miragea inference/account access and any chosen renderer/agent charges. No verified zero-cost synthesis entitlement. [1]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Line durations are measured, but word positions are estimated by syllable weighting and waveform pauses, not verified forced alignment. The provider receives selected text/reference audio. Cloned speech can take minutes; mocked tests and examples do not establish reliability.

How it uses AI

An open Node CLI generates one neural voice WAV per script line through Miragea, measures line durations, caches unchanged takes and writes frame manifests plus caption/subtitle files for Remotion or HyperFrames. [1]

Introduction

An open Node CLI generates one neural voice WAV per script line through Miragea, measures line durations, caches unchanged takes and writes frame manifests plus caption/subtitle files for Remotion or HyperFrames. [1]

What it is good for

Revising multi-voice narration without regenerating every line and handing actual audio durations to a code-based video timeline. [1]

Demo & examples

The repository links an unedited two-voice11.78-second example, its MP4/MP3, captions and frame manifest. Seventeen mocked-engine tests do not independently validate live synthesis or word alignment. [1]

Install

Use Node 18.17+ and the official GitHub npx package. Obtain a separate Miragea account/key and configure it locally. Remotion or HyperFrames is a separate rendering environment; ffprobe is only needed for non-WAV output. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use Node 18.17+ and the official GitHub npx package. Obtain a separate Miragea account/key and configure it locally. Remotion or HyperFrames is a separate rendering environment; ffprobe is only needed for non-WAV output.
npx github:RTY2345RR/remotion-voiceover --help
npx github:RTY2345RR/remotion-voiceover plan voiceover.md
npx github:RTY2345RR/remotion-voiceover generate voiceover.md --hyperframes

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Write scene headings, short lines and permitted voice IDs or authorized reference recordings in Markdown/JSON.
  2. Inspect plan first; generate only after selecting service terms and paid access.
  3. Load voiceover.json and captions.json into the video project; match the composition fps.
  4. Listen for pronunciation and repair drifting captions; split long lines. Editing a line reuses other cached audio.

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Node 18.17+; Miragea service access; separate Remotion/HyperFrames project. Optional ffprobe for MP3/non-WAV.
Platforms
Windows, macOS and Linux documented; exact OS-version support is not specified.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT client code; Miragea voices/model/service conditions, reference-voice consent and Remotion licensing remain separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT client code; Miragea voices/model/service conditions, reference-voice consent and Remotion licensing remain separate.
  • Cost: Separately billed Miragea inference/account access and any chosen renderer/agent charges. No verified zero-cost synthesis entitlement.

cd backend
python3 -m venv .venv
.venv/bin/python -m pip install -r requirements.txt
.venv/bin/python -m app.assets
.venv/bin/python -m uvicorn app.main:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8000
cd frontend
npm install
npm run dev

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Configure a model with sufficient context and test its connection.
  2. Upload permitted footage or provide an authorized supported source link; inspect transcription and proposed moments.
  3. Preview every clip, repair start/end, caption words and face/centre crop decisions.
  4. Render a short test, inspect sync/safe areas and export MP4. Keep the source and project state.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
About3 GB for Python packages and speech model plus source/output videos is documented; first speech download about 1.5 GB. GPU is optional, CPU fallback available. No universal minimum RAM/VRAM is given; the author’s2 GB figure concerns a selected large-v3 route, not all models.
Software
Python 3.10+, Node 20+, FFmpeg 6+ with libass, faster-whisper, MediaPipe and selected LLM access. Backend and frontend run separately.
Platforms
Windows setup and Mac/Linux environment commands are documented; uniform real-device validation is not established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT application. FFmpeg, yt-dlp, faster-whisper, MediaPipe, Poppins/OFL and each selected model/provider retain separate terms.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT application. FFmpeg, yt-dlp, faster-whisper, MediaPipe, Poppins/OFL and each selected model/provider retain separate terms.
  • Cost: Local compute plus separately billed selected LLM. A local model avoids a cloud per-call fee but needs its own hardware and weight permission; provider cost estimates are not verified quotes.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

Single-user local service has no accounts and stores provider keys in plaintext locally. Face tracking is tuned for talking heads; overlapping speakers, split screens and Hindi recognition can fail. Changed word counts receive redistributed timing. Heuristic clip selection is a test fallback, not the AI mode. [1]

How it uses AI

A local PDF/image editor integrates learned MI-GAN/LaMa inpainting, SlimSAM/SAM2 object extraction and PaddleOCR text separation with editable document layers. Telea erasing and booklet imposition are conventional operations, distinct from the AI modes. [1]

Introduction

A local PDF/image editor integrates learned MI-GAN/LaMa inpainting, SlimSAM/SAM2 object extraction and PaddleOCR text separation with editable document layers. Telea erasing and booklet imposition are conventional operations, distinct from the AI modes. [1]

What it is good for

Removing objects from permitted artwork, decomposing a flat poster into editable layers or preparing accessible editable document drafts. [1]

Demo & examples

The repository documents a synthetic poster browser test measuring a ball, logo, icons, curves and twelve text items. This is maintainer test evidence, not independent fidelity or redaction-security validation. [1]

Install

For the web route, obtain the source, run npm install and npm run dev, then open the displayed local HTTP address. Production build writes dist for static hosting. The optional Tauri2 desktop shell has a separate Rust/native build setup. [1]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  2. Choose AI erasing, Magic Layers or object extraction deliberately; review the first-download prompt and the model terms.
  3. Inspect masks, replacement pixels, OCR/font approximations and layer ordering at original resolution.
  4. Export a test, reopen it and inspect actual contents; do not equate a visual cover with validated removal of confidential text.

Node/npm for web development, a browser and ONNX Runtime Web via WebGPU/WASM. Optional Tauri2 needs its native build toolchain. Minimum Node/browser versions are not specified.
Platforms
Browser route plus documented Windows/macOS/Linux Tauri targets; this source review does not verify each packaged OS route.

Reconstruction additionally installs requirements-4dgs.txt without build isolation; GUI requirements are separate. [1] [3]

pip install --no-build-isolation -r requirements-4dgs.txt

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Use clear1080p+9:16 footage with at least121 frames, one visible full/upper body and no large camera movement.
  2. Start with a compact six-view inference job and inspect person, motion and generated unseen views before increasing coverage.
  3. Point reconstruct.py at the generated result and a distinct output directory; inspect run/config/training logs and saved checkpoints.
  4. Load latest.npz with reconstruct.py view on loopback7861, or render a360-degree orbit; inspect artifacts and consent/rights before reuse.

Hardware
The author reports22 GB CUDA peak and27 seconds per121-frame video on one RTX4090 for an inference setting. Total system RAM/storage and universal reconstruction VRAM requirements are not documented; training batch size can be reduced but this does not prove a lower minimum.
Software
Python 3.11 Conda environment, CUDA-compatible PyTorch/dependencies, initialized GVHMR, selected body/generative models; separate4DGS and optional GUI requirements.
Platforms
A CUDA GPU workflow is documented. The reviewed sources do not establish a Windows/Mac or exact Linux-version support matrix.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  4. Inspect the imported metadata and draft timeline. Keep the sibling library backed up and check paths before using the project on another Mac.

First project

  4. Keep the editable score and samples. In a live session review proposals before accepting, and export a new listening bundle without overwriting older work.

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Mono processing; latency can be nonzero when rates differ. Quality scaling affects only supporting models. Current source warns that switching to an uninitialized submodel can make a quality change unsafe for the audio thread; owner smooth-switching claims are not a universal artifact-free guarantee. The build script follows the latest cross-toolchain rather than documenting a pinned reproduced toolchain.

How it uses AI

A Max/MSP external loads actual NAM and AIDA-X neural amplifier models, processes mono audio through NeuralAudio and resamples between model and host rates. It exposes model-load/status and supported quality controls to a patch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

A Max/MSP external loads actual NAM and AIDA-X neural amplifier models, processes mono audio through NeuralAudio and resamples between model and host rates. It exposes model-load/status and supported quality controls to a patch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Using an individually licensed neural amp capture in an editable Max or Max for Live performance/sound-design patch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Review those side effects before using it; it was not run here. [1] [3] [4] [5]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Place the matching external in the intended Max package/search path and start with a controlled mono audio level.
  2. Send load with the path to a permitted .nam or AIDA-X .json/.aidax capture; check loaded/error/queued and latency messages.
  3. For NAM, prewarm before performance. Query quality_supported and adjust quality only on a model that supports it.
  4. Audition bypass/model output and changes at conservative levels, check sample-rate conversion delay, and retain the patch and exact permitted model revision.

The repository was created on 2026-11-11. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 0 GitHub stars · Documentation review; installation not tested

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Exploring an editable, deliberately limited learned timbre instrument from owned oud/saz recordings on a trusted single-user machine.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: Included demonstration sounds are procedural synthetic fixtures, not proof of a pretrained realistic oud/saz model. Current source implements a conditioned10→96→96→24 harmonic MLP; the older v0.5 manual describes a3→64 baseline, so documentation is not fully synchronized with the implementation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Use the current checkout and its documented local or neural-container setup. Main README/user manual require Node 22+, while the local-development guide says Node 20+; use the stricter documented22+ route and keep the inconsistency visible. Local backend development uses Python 3.10+ and separate neural requirements with PyTorch2.3–<3. Configure a private AUTH_SECRET and keep services restricted to trusted local access. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT original software. PyTorch/runtime terms and every supplied recording/instrument pack remain separate; recording ownership is not obtained from the code license. No paid inference service is required by the selected local baseline. Local compute/storage and owned recording equipment/material. Public-service security and production hardening remain unfinished; this guide assesses trusted local experimentation and completes an older screened record. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. The network predicts24 harmonic amplitudes, with conditioned note/velocity/time/cents/articulation; some envelopes and articulation effects are still procedural. Unrepresented pitches/techniques are extrapolations, and realistic attacks, noise, transitions or acoustic fidelity are not established. Neural rendering explicitly loads on CPU; no universal RAM/VRAM/storage minimum is documented. Older manuals describe in-memory datasets, while newer library-linked records persist per account; do not assume all jobs, browser storage and training paths are safely isolated for multiuser/public deployment.

How it uses AI

A local instrument studio trains a small PyTorch harmonic-envelope model from permitted note recordings and renders generated WAVs for sample mapping and composition. Conventional additive demo synthesis and recording meters are separate from this learned route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

A local instrument studio trains a small PyTorch harmonic-envelope model from permitted note recordings and renders generated WAVs for sample mapping and composition. Conventional additive demo synthesis and recording meters are separate from this learned route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Exploring an editable, deliberately limited learned timbre instrument from owned oud/saz recordings on a trusted single-user machine. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

Included demonstration sounds are procedural synthetic fixtures, not proof of a pretrained realistic oud/saz model. Current source implements a conditioned10→96→96→24 harmonic MLP; the older v0.5 manual describes a3→64 baseline, so documentation is not fully synchronized with the implementation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

Use the current checkout and its documented local or neural-container setup. Main README/user manual require Node 22+, while the local-development guide says Node 20+; use the stricter documented22+ route and keep the inconsistency visible. Local backend development uses Python 3.10+ and separate neural requirements with PyTorch2.3–<3. Configure a private AUTH_SECRET and keep services restricted to trusted local access. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the current checkout and its documented local or neural-container setup. Main README/user manual require Node 22+, while the local-development guide says Node 20+; use the stricter documented22+ route and keep the inconsistency visible. Local backend development uses Python 3.10+ and separate neural requirements with PyTorch2.3–<3. Configure a private AUTH_SECRET and keep services restricted to trusted local access.
git clone https://github.com/panosadamop/OpenTimbre.git
cd OpenTimbre
docker compose -f docker-compose.neural.yml up --build

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Open the local Studio, create the intended account and instrument library, and retain original WAV masters outside browser storage.
  2. Upload at least two permitted short uncompressed16/32-bit PCM WAVs with correct MIDI pitch, velocity and rights declaration; link them to the desired oud/saz library.
  3. Train the local model and retain its model ID/metadata. Generate a short Local Neural WAV and listen for pitch, decay and timbre errors.
  4. Import the reviewed result into the sampler/library and export JSON plus WAV backups. Use Song JSON/WAV where microtonal fidelity matters; basic MIDI does not preserve per-note cents.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Node 22+ in the main setup; Python 3.10+ for native backend, PyTorch>=2.3,<3 for neural training, or Docker Compose neural configuration. Correctly labeled owned recordings are required; no realistic pretrained instrument is bundled.
Platforms
Windows PowerShell and macOS/Linux development scripts are documented. They do not establish independently tested training/device compatibility; the selected PyTorch build and browser audio support remain relevant.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  • Weights: MIT original software. PyTorch/runtime terms and every supplied recording/instrument pack remain separate; recording ownership is not obtained from the code license. No paid inference service is required by the selected local baseline.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT original software. PyTorch/runtime terms and every supplied recording/instrument pack remain separate; recording ownership is not obtained from the code license. No paid inference service is required by the selected local baseline.
  • Cost: Local compute/storage and owned recording equipment/material. Public-service security and production hardening remain unfinished; this guide assesses trusted local experimentation and completes an older screened record.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Limitations

The network predicts24 harmonic amplitudes, with conditioned note/velocity/time/cents/articulation; some envelopes and articulation effects are still procedural. Unrepresented pitches/techniques are extrapolations, and realistic attacks, noise, transitions or acoustic fidelity are not established. Neural rendering explicitly loads on CPU; no universal RAM/VRAM/storage minimum is documented. Older manuals describe in-memory datasets, while newer library-linked records persist per account; do not assume all jobs, browser storage and training paths are safely isolated for multiuser/public deployment. How it uses AI

Learned stem separation, beat/chord recognition and instrument transcription feed deterministic quantization and notation, producing editable per-instrument and combined MuseScore scores from permitted audio/video. [1]

Introduction

Learned stem separation, beat/chord recognition and instrument transcription feed deterministic quantization and notation, producing editable per-instrument and combined MuseScore scores from permitted audio/video. [1]

What it is good for

Drafting a multi-instrument score and identifying bars that require listening and manual correction. [1]

Demo & examples

The documented pipeline produces MuseScore/MusicXML/PDF/WAV parts and a report of bars to inspect. GPU-dependent synthetic tests and owner descriptions are not independent transcription-accuracy validation. [1]

Install

Use the documented Linux/NVIDIA CUDA environment with uv, FFmpeg/ffprobe, Git and MuseScore4 Flatpak. Review all selected model permissions before running the source setup script; it fetches pinned vendor code and separate environments/caches. MuScriptor weights are gated and noncommercial; the default BS-RoFormer SW weights have no declared license in the reviewed source, so permission remains unresolved. [1]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Choose permitted source audio and a model route whose weight permissions you have resolved.
  2. Run a small test and inspect beat/meter/key, chord chart and separated stems before trusting notation.
  3. Select instruments explicitly when automatic selection is wrong; use per-song YAML/tool overrides for a supported alternative.
  4. Resume individual steps where useful, then inspect every instrumental part and full score in MuseScore against the recording and report.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Linux, NVIDIA CUDA GPU, uv, FFmpeg/ffprobe, Git and MuseScore4 via Flatpak; selected vendor environments and gated model access. The GPU-only models deliberately fail without a GPU.
Platforms
The declared workflow is Linux with NVIDIA/CUDA. A CPU fallback or Mac/Windows setup is not documented.

How it uses AI

A local language model produces a plan and schema-constrained volumetric DSL; deterministic Python converts voxels to real LDraw catalog parts, checks geometry and creates a shopping list, instructions and Blender renders. [1]

Introduction

A local language model produces a plan and schema-constrained volumetric DSL; deterministic Python converts voxels to real LDraw catalog parts, checks geometry and creates a shopping list, instructions and Blender renders. [1]

What it is good for

Drafting an inspectable toy-brick sculpture from a French-language brief and checking the generated part/layout plan before any physical build. [1]

Demo & examples

The owner publishes castle, tree and car examples and a geometry evaluation. Those are not an independent physical assembly, structural-strength or part-availability certification. [1]

Install

Use Python 3.11+, LM Studio with its local server on localhost:1234, Blender, a separately obtained LDraw library and ImportLDraw. Install requirements in a chosen environment and inspect tools/check_env.py output before a small CLI generation. The web route additionally has FastAPI/PostgreSQL/Vue requirements described in the full guide. [1]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Write a compact original shape brief and choose a compatible locally served model with reviewed terms.
  2. Inspect the LLM plan/DSL and its bounded geometry-repair attempts; simple shapes and buildings are documented as more reliable.
  3. Review the timestamped output folder, LDraw part choices, assembly instructions and renders.
  4. Check inventory, stability, connection choices and physical constraints independently before obtaining parts or building.

Video to Guitar Score

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Reconstructing a reviewable guitar-score draft from legible on-screen TAB, with human decisions on ambiguous measures.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

Use Python 3.10+ and requirements.txt, plus FFmpeg for frame extraction and Tesseract for the portable OCR-candidate route. Optional Apple Vision Swift/Objective-C accelerators are Mac-only and are not the sole supported route. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. This reads visible notation; it does not invent a score from performance audio or hand movements. Low-resolution/incomplete TAB, OCR mistakes, rhythm interpretation and missing source measures remain human review problems. A validation report is not independent musical correctness.

How it uses AI

An AI vision agent recovers notation/TAB visibly displayed in an authorized video, using extracted frames and OCR candidates while retaining per-event visual provenance and editable Guitar Pro score outputs. [1]

Introduction

An AI vision agent recovers notation/TAB visibly displayed in an authorized video, using extracted frames and OCR candidates while retaining per-event visual provenance and editable Guitar Pro score outputs. [1]

What it is good for

Reconstructing a reviewable guitar-score draft from legible on-screen TAB, with human decisions on ambiguous measures. [1]

Demo & examples

The README shows a playable-score example and specifies full-score validation before a final label. The screenshot does not establish independent note accuracy or transcription from audio alone. [1]

Install

Import the complete repository as a skill through the chosen AI host’s supported workflow. Use Python 3.10+ and requirements.txt, plus FFmpeg for frame extraction and Tesseract for the portable OCR-candidate route. Optional Apple Vision Swift/Objective-C accelerators are Mac-only and are not the sole supported route. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Import the complete repository as a skill through the chosen AI host’s supported workflow. Use Python 3.10+ and requirements.txt, plus FFmpeg for frame extraction and Tesseract for the portable OCR-candidate route. Optional Apple Vision Swift/Objective-C accelerators are Mac-only and are not the sole supported route.
python -m pip install -r requirements.txt

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Provide a permitted local file or video link with clearly visible notation, and choose standard or strict measure-by-measure review.
  2. Have the agent extract source frames and build an event ledger, preserving the video’s actual string/fret choices.
  3. Treat OCR as candidates and visually verify all string/fret values; resolve ambiguous or incomplete measures with the user.
  4. Validate the whole editable score, open/play it in a compatible notation application and retain rendered review pages plus the source ledger.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Python 3.10+, listed Python dependencies, FFmpeg, Tesseract and a capable AI-agent/vision host. A compatible Guitar Pro application or other chosen score viewer is separate; minimum memory/storage and exact host versions are not documented.
Platforms
The main Python/Tesseract workflow declares Mac, Windows and Linux support. Apple Vision utilities are optional macOS accelerators; uniform end-to-end platform reproduction is not independently established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT skill/scripts. AI-host access, OCR/model data, score-format libraries, a chosen proprietary notation host and rights in the video/music are separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT skill/scripts. AI-host access, OCR/model data, score-format libraries, a chosen proprietary notation host and rights in the video/music are separate.
  • Cost: Local extraction/OCR plus chosen AI-host and notation-application access. This guide completes an earlier screened entry without running its scripts.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

This reads visible notation; it does not invent a score from performance audio or hand movements. Low-resolution/incomplete TAB, OCR mistakes, rhythm interpretation and missing source measures remain human review problems. A validation report is not independent musical correctness. [1]

Scope: Creating editable original song/underscore sketches from an approved template, then reviewing arrangement, sound and measured mix issues.

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. The sound comes from the template: the tool does not load new plugins, change presets or automate mixer controls. FLP is undocumented and a future FL update can break compatibility. Loudness, true-peak and band metrics are conventional diagnostics and do not judge musical taste. Optional film mode may select a licensed stock track for shorter videos; that is not always newly composed music.

How it uses AI

An AI agent or OpenAI-compatible model authors a structured song specification. Open Python tooling writes editable FLP/MIDI files; a licensed FL Studio installation renders them, while conventional loudness/balance analysis feeds revisions back to the agent. [1]

Introduction

An AI agent or OpenAI-compatible model authors a structured song specification. Open Python tooling writes editable FLP/MIDI files; a licensed FL Studio installation renders them, while conventional loudness/balance analysis feeds revisions back to the agent. [1]

What it is good for

Creating editable original song/underscore sketches from an approved template, then reviewing arrangement, sound and measured mix issues. [1]

Demo & examples

Three example-spec songs were rendered by the owner with FL Studio2026 stock template instruments. Owner20/40-second timings and automatic mix-fix claims are not independently reproduced performance or taste judgments. [1]

Install

Use Python 3.10+ and the documented Git package. Building FLP/MIDI works across operating systems; rendering requires Windows, licensed FL Studio20.7+ and FFmpeg. The author reports format/render tests through2026. Inspect available templates/channels before building. Register the optional MCP server or configure a separately entitled compatible model through the local Studio. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use Python 3.10+ and the documented Git package. Building FLP/MIDI works across operating systems; rendering requires Windows, licensed FL Studio20.7+ and FFmpeg. The author reports format/render tests through2026. Inspect available templates/channels before building. Register the optional MCP server or configure a separately entitled compatible model through the local Studio.
pip install "project5pi-midi-for-claude[mcp] @ git+https://github.com/ProjectFlowmar/project5pi-midi-for-claude"

p5midi inspect HipHop-Trap
p5midi make examples/night-drive-trap.json -o out/night-drive.flp

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Choose or prepare an owned licensed FL Studio template with the desired instrument and mixer sound.
  2. Have the agent author a song JSON with tempo, harmony, parts and arrangement; inspect channel names and notes.
  3. Close FL Studio before command-line rendering, then inspect generated FLP/MIDI/WAV/MP3 and the analysis report.
  4. Listen and revise manually or through the agent, preserving versions. Export aligned stems for a separate mix if useful.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Python 3.10+; rendering additionally requires Windows, FL Studio20.7+ and FFmpeg. Optional MCP client or separately configured OpenAI-compatible model. Exact universal RAM/storage requirements are not documented.
Platforms
Project/MIDI building is documented across platforms; FL command-line rendering is Windows-only. No native Mac/Linux complete render route is established by the source.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT Python tooling, shipping no Image-Line files. FL Studio, its templates/plugins, provider services, selected weights and any stock tracks retain separate terms. The hosted Windows product/free allowance is distinct from running this open source with your own host/key.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT Python tooling, shipping no Image-Line files. FL Studio, its templates/plugins, provider services, selected weights and any stock tracks retain separate terms. The hosted Windows product/free allowance is distinct from running this open source with your own host/key.
  • Cost: Licensed FL Studio and local compute, plus separately billed selected model/service. Provider per-song estimates and a hosted free tier are not verified current prices. This completes an older screened record.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

The sound comes from the template: the tool does not load new plugins, change presets or automate mixer controls. FLP is undocumented and a future FL update can break compatibility. Loudness, true-peak and band metrics are conventional diagnostics and do not judge musical taste. Optional film mode may select a licensed stock track for shorter videos; that is not always newly composed music. [1]

Open WebUI image models via llama-swap

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · first-profile

First complete guide in this library, including completion of any earlier screened entry; not a claim of a launch today.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 6 GitHub stars · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Using a configured local image-generation workflow from a chat interface while keeping model-specific graphs and backend choices inspectable.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The owner publishes model-selector screenshots, and an independent user reports several days of successful default image generation with supplied custom workflows. That supports first-hand use, not independently assessed image quality or every later custom/multi-image workflow. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Prepare Open WebUI0.5+ and llama-swap with the intended image backend reachable through /upstream/<name>. In the Open WebUI admin Functions panel, create a function, paste the official llamaswap_image_models.py source, save and enable it. Configure its Valves and actual backend model files/workflows. It uses aiohttp/pydantic already bundled by the host; model/backend setup remains separate. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · documented: Inspected the titles/states in up to ten recently updated issue/PR records and selected first-hand report bodies; 3 records returned. This bounded sample, owner PRs and repository activity do not establish sustained support, closed blockers or independent reliability. [4]
  • Independent use · verified: An independent user reports several days of actual default-setup use and working image generation, supplies their custom workflow files, and describes hardcoding/documentation and GPU model-unload limitations. This verifies first-hand use of the default bridge, not all custom nodes, formats or output quality. [3]
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT function code. Open WebUI, llama-swap, ComfyUI/custom nodes, exact image/video checkpoints and any remote provider retain separate terms. No model rights or compute credits are supplied by the function. Local compute/models where configured, plus any chosen remote provider or host obligations. Heavy-model duration and API/storage costs are not universally verified. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. One-GPU coordination is configuration-dependent, not an out-of-memory guarantee. An earlier user reported that a busy ComfyUI could defeat unloading; current docs offer a queue-drain/memory-check cmdStop recipe, which was not independently stress-tested here. Sending another prompt in the same chat can abandon an in-flight render. File-store uploads accumulate; inline-base64 fallback can make later chat messages large. Remote backends receive their configured inputs.

How it uses AI

An Open WebUI manifold function exposes selected ComfyUI or OpenAI-compatible image backends as model-dropdown entries, translates prompts/references into workflow requests and stores returned images/video in the chat file store. [1] [3]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Configure LLAMASWAP_URL and MODELS_JSON using a supported built-in template or an inspected custom API graph, with file names matching the actual server.
  2. Select an image-model entry and start with a short text-to-image request; inspect returned media and errors.
  3. For image editing, attach permitted references in the expected order and use a suitable image-input workflow; missing numbered inputs deliberately fail.
  4. Check GPU coordination and file-store behavior, keep separate chats for parallel requests, and back up outputs before any host-side pruning.

Mascot Booth

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-11-10. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-16 · 0 GitHub stars · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Making a short talking clip from your own mascot art and permitted recording, or preparing an editable presenter for a larger agent-authored film.

Evidence checked 2026-11-16; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The owner shows pose/mouth examples and reports timing measurements on one85-second Taglish take and selected render-sync tests. Those compare internal/provider timestamps and are not independent phoneme accuracy, cross-language or visual-quality validation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: For a clip, use the official static web app in desktop Chrome/Edge. Supply your own six-pose sheet, correct face boxes and permitted audio. No engine installation is required. A local static copy uses Node 20+ and tools/serve-app.mjs; replace reserved branded assets before hosting/distributing a copy. The optional full-film engine additionally needs Git, FFmpeg and node install.mjs, which installs dependencies/agent skills and downloads headless Chrome. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT code, with separate MIT muxer/OFL font notices. Cap Zeke art, logos/icons and sample voice are all rights reserved and must not become your presenter/product; replace them in hosted/distributed copies. Runtime speech conversions/weights, ElevenLabs, AI-host services and supplied character/audio rights remain separate. Supplied/recorded audio can use local processing after model downloads; optional ElevenLabs uses the owner’s separate account/credits. AI pose creation or an agent host can have separate costs. This completes an older screened entry. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Without learned alignment or provided timings, mouth motion follows audio loudness/brightness, which is conventional animation. Owner no-upload claims concern the local processing route: external pose generation and optional ElevenLabs send their specified inputs to those services. The inspected worker downloads speech libraries/models; this was not a complete network/security audit. English/Taglish UI and one Taglish example do not guarantee all-language alignment. Auto-transcription and supplied transcripts still need correction.

How it uses AI

A browser app uses a downloaded wav2vec2 speech model for letter-level forced alignment, with optional Whisper transcription, to drive a drawn mascot’s mouth shapes. Pose cutting/warping and video encoding are conventional; an optional AI-agent engine authors complete cue-based explainer films. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

A browser app uses a downloaded wav2vec2 speech model for letter-level forced alignment, with optional Whisper transcription, to drive a drawn mascot’s mouth shapes. Pose cutting/warping and video encoding are conventional; an optional AI-agent engine authors complete cue-based explainer films. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Making a short talking clip from your own mascot art and permitted recording, or preparing an editable presenter for a larger agent-authored film. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The owner shows pose/mouth examples and reports timing measurements on one85-second Taglish take and selected render-sync tests. Those compare internal/provider timestamps and are not independent phoneme accuracy, cross-language or visual-quality validation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

For a clip, use the official static web app in desktop Chrome/Edge. Supply your own six-pose sheet, correct face boxes and permitted audio. No engine installation is required. A local static copy uses Node 20+ and tools/serve-app.mjs; replace reserved branded assets before hosting/distributing a copy. The optional full-film engine additionally needs Git, FFmpeg and node install.mjs, which installs dependencies/agent skills and downloads headless Chrome. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Prepare exactly six separated consistent-scale poses of your own character, using authorized drawn art or a separately chosen AI image host.
  2. Import the sheet, correct mouth/eye boxes and optionally supply separately drawn mouth shapes.
  3. Upload/record permitted speech and enable Sharper lip sync with a correct transcript where possible; inspect alignments and every mouth/pose transition.
  4. Export transparent WebM or background MP4 in a supported browser. For the optional engine, retain presenter assets, cue sheets and editable film code, and inspect the full render rather than relying only on its sync check.

Platforms
The browser route declares desktop Chrome/Edge export; that does not establish every device/OS combination. The optional Node engine declares Windows, macOS and Linux support; this was not independently reproduced.
